埃博拉:20名接触者在肯尼亚被解除隔离,刚果民主共和国充满“希望” - 联合国新闻

Ebola: 20 contacts released from quarantine in Kenya, amid vaccine ‘hope’ in DR Congo - UN News
新闻简报在肯尼亚,与该国首例本迪布焦病毒病相关的 20 名人员在完成 21 天的监测期后于 2026 年 10 月 27 日解除隔离。这些接触者仍然没有症状,并在出院前接受了健康评估、咨询和随访。肯尼亚于 2026 年 10 月 6 日确认了第一例病例,涉及一名从刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 返回的男子,后来在内罗毕一家医院死亡。卫生部截至 2026 年 10 月 22 日追踪了所有接触者,包括国外接触者。八名接触者出现症状,埃博拉病毒检测呈阳性,但没有社区传播的报告。世界卫生组织赞扬肯尼亚的卫生应对措施,并将继续在 监测、检测和风险沟通方面支持该国。与此同时,刚果民主共和国正在开展针对 Bundibugyo 病毒病的 疫苗 试验,TUMANI 平台评估多个 疫苗 候选药物。该试验以斯瓦希里语中的“希望”一词命名,旨在评估 疫苗 对 Bundibugyo 菌株的功效,但结果尚未公布。世卫组织还强调了早期检测和支持性护理对于提高康复机会的重要性,因为尚无针对本迪布焦病毒病的许可 疫苗 或 治疗。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年10月27日(发布日期)、2026年10月6日(活动日期)、2026年10月22日(隔离安置日期)。
地点与对象
肯尼亚,在肯尼亚和国外追踪到了接触者,包括家庭成员、航空公司乘客、机组人员、机场工作人员和医务人员。
事件
据联合国世界卫生组织 (WHO) 和肯尼亚卫生部报告,肯尼亚在完成首例本迪布焦病毒病病例后,在完成 21 天的监测期后,解除了 20 名接触者的隔离。
规模、影响与风险
肯尼亚报告了九例埃博拉确诊病例和两人死亡,没有社区传播的证据。另有 98 名接触者仍在隔离中,其中 8 名接触者出现症状,并通过实验室检测证实埃博拉病毒呈阳性。没有明确指出对社区有风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织和肯尼亚当局开展了接触者追踪、实验室检测和检疫程序。世卫组织代表强调,被释放的人不存在感染风险。刚果民主共和国正在针对 Bundibugyo 病毒病进行 疫苗 试验,结果正在等待中。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: 20 contacts released from quarantine in Kenya, amid vaccine ‘hope’ in DR Congo - UN News

News Brief
In Kenya, 20 individuals associated with the country’s first case of Bundibugyo virus disease were released from quarantine on 27 October 2026 after completing a 21-day monitoring period. These contacts remained asymptomatic and underwent health assessments, counseling, and follow-up before being discharged. Kenya confirmed its first case on 6 October 2026, involving a man who returned from the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and later died in a Nairobi hospital. The Ministry of Health traced all contacts, including those abroad, by 22 October 2026. Eight contacts developed symptoms and tested positive for Ebola disease, but no community spread was reported. The WHO commended Kenya’s health response and continues to support the country in surveillance, testing, and risk communication. Meanwhile, the DRC is conducting a vaccine trial for Bundibugyo virus disease, with the TUMAINI platform evaluating multiple vaccine candidates. The trial, named after the Swahili word for 'hope,' aims to assess vaccine efficacy against the Bundibugyo strain, though results are not yet available. The WHO also highlighted the importance of early detection and supportive care in improving recovery chances, as no licensed vaccine or treatment exists for Bundibugyo virus disease.
Original source text (English)

Facebook Twitter Print Email Ebola: 20 contacts released from quarantine in Kenya, amid vaccine ‘hope’ in DR Congo 27 October 2026 Health Twenty people associated with Kenya’s first case of Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease have been reunited with their families after being released from quarantine following completion of a mandatory 21-day monitoring period. They were discharged on Monday, according to the UN World Health Organization (WHO), which has been assisting the Kenyan authorities with Ebola preparedness since the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) declared an outbreak on 15 May. Another 98 contacts remain in quarantine. No risk to the community The people who are now back at home remained asymptomatic during quarantine and underwent standard protocols, including a final health assessment, counselling on post-discharge precautions and follow-up where required before being released. “We ask communities to welcome them home warmly,” said Dr Patrick Amoth, Kenya’s director general for health. “Having completed 21 days without symptoms, they pose no risk of infection to anyone.” The WHO representative to Kenya, Dr Neema Rusibamayila Kimambo, echoed his message. “People who complete quarantine have helped protect their families, their communities and the whole country,” she said. Rush to trace contacts Kenya confirmed its first Ebola case on 6 October – a Kenyan man who had been working in the DRC, where the caseload is approaching the 10,000 mark. He flew back to Kenya via Uganda on 3 October and died in a Nairobi hospital two days later. The Ministry of Health conducted robust contact tracing of anyone who may have been exposed, including family members, airline passengers and crew, airport workers and medical personnel. By 22 October, all contacts were traced – both in Kenya and abroad – and placed in quarantine. As of Monday, eight contacts had developed symptoms and were confirmed positive for Ebola disease through laboratory testing. They were immediately transferred to the Ebola treatment centre in Nairobi. Praise and support In total, Kenya has reported nine confirmed Ebola cases and two deaths. All are part of one connected group and there is no evidence that the virus has spread to the community. “WHO commends the Ministry of Health, the Kenya National Public Health Institute, national and county health teams for their work in responding quickly to every new case,” said Dr. Kimambo. The UN agency continues to support Kenya in all aspects of Ebola response, including coordination, disease surveillance, contact tracing, laboratory testing, case management, infection prevention and control, health screening at points of entry, risk communication and community engagement. Although there is no licensed vaccine or specific treatment for Bundibugyo virus disease, WHO stressed that early detection and supportive care improve chances of recovery. DRC vaccine trial Meanwhile, “a crucial clinical trial” was launched in the DRC on Friday to determine if vaccines can provide protection against the disease, said WHO’s Dr. Meg Doherty, speaking from the eastern city of Bunia to journalists in Geneva. The TUMAINI trial platform is designed so that several vaccine candidates can be evaluated within one research framework using a strategy called the ring vaccination approach involving contacts of people with a confirmed case of Ebola. “Tumaini actually means ‘hope’ in Swahili, and is giving us some hope,” said Dr. Doherty, director of WHO’s Department of Science for Health. Soundcloud A moving experience The first vaccine under evaluation is Ervebo which has been effective against Ebola caused by the Zaire virus. It is not yet known if it will have any protective effect against the Bundibugyo strain. Dr. Doherty attended the launch, where four people were vaccinated. “It was moving to see this trial begin, just as it has been deeply sad to see the effect of this outbreak on communities,” she said. “I lived and worked in DRC nearly 40 years ago, and it is quite heartbreaking to see this beautiful country challenged by outbreaks on top of humanitarian crises.” Evaluations and recommendations Last week, WHO convened technical advisory groups that evaluated the evidence on two vaccines designed specifically against Bundibugyo virus: ChAdOx1, developed by the University of Oxford with the Serum Institute of India, and mRNA-1469 developed by the American company Moderna. Recommendations will be published this week. “In the meantime, rings are being set up, with people who have a family member sick with Ebola, or who have been exposed to the virus another way,” she said, noting that two rings are currently in place and several more are being prepared. “Community members are already walking into the site asking to be enrolled, highlighting the intense need for an efficacious vaccine,” she added. ‘An important step’ Dr. Doherty explained that a decade ago, WHO and partners used the ring vaccination approach in Guinea during the West African Ebola outbreak to help establish the efficacy of the Ervebo vaccine. “Today, scientists are building on that experience to seek answers about the Bundibugyo virus,” she said. The trial is led by the Congolese authorities and is integrated into the ongoing response to the outbreak. It is being managed by the National Public Health and Biomedical Research Institute, known by the French acronym IRNRB. “We do not yet know for certain when results will begin to emerge. We expect it will take several months,” she said. “But already, having this trial in place is an important step towards gathering scientific data that will help policy makers reach decisions on vaccine use, and we anticipate, to protect people in this outbreak and future outbreaks.” Dr Doherty noted that several other vaccines are under development, “and as they go through their initial studies, if they look promising, they too could be brought into this vaccine platform trial.” Ebola Virus Disease Facebook Twitter Print Email Back to top

Event
Kenya released 20 contacts from quarantine after completing a 21-day monitoring period following its first case of Bundibugyo virus disease, reported by the UN World Health Organization (WHO) and Kenya’s Ministry of Health.
Scale, impact and risk
Kenya reported nine confirmed Ebola cases and two deaths, with no evidence of community spread. Another 98 contacts remain in quarantine, and eight contacts developed symptoms and were confirmed positive for Ebola disease through laboratory testing. No risk to the community was explicitly stated.
Response, status and uncertainty
WHO and Kenyan authorities conducted contact tracing, laboratory testing, and quarantine protocols. The WHO representative emphasized that the people released pose no risk of infection. The DRC is conducting a vaccine trial for Bundibugyo virus disease, and results are pending.

1号受试者在针对刚果民主共和国后方毒株暴发的埃博拉疫苗试验中接种以预防疾病 - ABC新闻 - 突发新闻,最新新闻和视频

1st person inoculated in trial for Ebola vaccine to protect against strain behind DRC outbreak - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 27 日,第一人在针对本迪布焦埃博拉病毒的 疫苗 临床试验中接种了疫苗,该病毒是刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 正在发生的 暴发 的罪魁祸首。这项名为“Tumaini”的试验由刚果民主共和国国家生物医学研究所和比利时安特卫普大学全球健康研究所进行,并得到无国界医生组织 (MSF) 的支持。该试验的参与者包括过去 21 天内新确诊的本迪布焦病毒病例的接触者。DRC 暴发 已导致 10,000 多例病例和近 5,000 人死亡,目前尚无针对该菌株的 疫苗 或 治疗。该试验旨在评估几种 疫苗 候选药物的安全性、有效性和免疫原性,其中包括针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒开发的 ERVEBO。独立的数据监测委员会将审查试验数据,并且可能会根据其有效性添加或终止其他 疫苗 候选药物。将儿童和孕妇纳入试验是解决儿童和孕妇在临床研究中代表性不足问题的重要一步。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 10 月 27 日,第一个人在埃博拉 疫苗 临床试验中接种。该试验的时间表包括第 1 天和第 29 天,参与者在第 1 天接受 疫苗 候选药物或标准 治疗,所有参与者在第 29 天接受替代干预。
地点与对象
该临床试验正在刚果民主共和国(DRC),特别是伊图里进行。参与者是入组前 21 天内新确诊的本迪布焦病毒病患者的接触者。
事件
第一个人在针对本迪布焦埃博拉病毒的 疫苗 临床试验中接种了疫苗,该病毒是刚果民主共和国正在进行的 暴发 的罪魁祸首。该试验由刚果民主共和国国家生物医学研究所和比利时安特卫普大学全球健康研究所进行,并得到无国界医生组织 (MSF) 的支持。
规模、影响与风险
由本迪布焦埃博拉病毒引起的刚果民主共和国 暴发 已导致超过 10,000 例病例和近 5,000 人死亡。目前没有可用于 Bundibugyo 病毒的 疫苗 或 治疗。该试验旨在评估几种 疫苗 候选药物的安全性、有效性和免疫原性,其中包括专为扎伊尔埃博拉病毒设计的 ERVEBO。
应对、状态与不确定性
Tumaini 临床试验正在评估多种 疫苗 Bundibugyo 病毒候选药物。独立的数据监测委员会将审查试验数据,并将根据其有效性添加或终止其他 疫苗 候选药物。该试验包括儿童和孕妇,这对于 暴发 反应具有重要意义。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

1st person inoculated in trial for Ebola vaccine to protect against strain behind DRC outbreak - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
On October 27, 2026, the first person was inoculated in a clinical trial for a vaccine targeting the Bundibugyo ebolavirus, which is responsible for the ongoing outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The trial, known as Tumaini, is being conducted by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale in the DRC and the University of Antwerp's Global Health Institute in Belgium, with support from Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF). The trial includes participants who are contacts of newly confirmed Bundibugyo virus cases within the previous 21 days. The DRC outbreak has led to over 10,000 cases and nearly 5,000 deaths, and currently, there are no vaccines or treatments available for this strain. The trial aims to assess the safety, efficacy, and immunogenicity of several vaccine candidates, including ERVEBO, which was developed for Zaire ebolavirus. An independent data monitoring committee will review the trial data, and additional vaccine candidates may be added or discontinued based on their effectiveness. The inclusion of children and pregnant women in the trial is a notable step toward addressing their underrepresentation in clinical research.

OffOn Stream on 1st person inoculated in trial for Ebola vaccine to protect against strain behind DRC outbreak The trial will test multiple vaccine candidates. ByMary Kekatos October 27, 2026, 8:44 PM Share The first person has been inoculated in a clinical trial for a vaccine that could help protect against the viral strain behind the Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), according to Médecins Sans Frontières (Doctors Without Borders). Bundibugyo ebolavirus (BDBV), a rare variant of Ebola, is behind the DRC outbreak that has led to more than 10,000 cases and nearly 5,000 deaths, the World Health Organization Regional Office for Africa posted Tuesday. There are no currently no vaccines or treatments available to treat Bundibugyo virus. The Tumaini clinical trial, as it's known, is evaluating the safety, efficacy and immunogenicity -- meaning the ability to generate an immune response -- of several vaccine candidates. A health worker collects a blood sample from a patient before administering a dose of the Tumaini vaccine against Ebola virus, in Ituri, Congo, Oct. 23, 2026. Dieudonne Dirole/AP Photo Related What we know about the spread of Ebola amid growing outbreak in DRC The trial is being co-sponsored and co-led by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale in the DRC and the University of Antwerp's Global Health Institute in Belgium. Partners include the Congo Ministry of Health, Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF), the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI), Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention, the World Health Organization, and the Institute of Tropical Medicine in Antwerp. Participants are made up of people who at the time of enrollment were contacts within the previous 21 days of a patient who is a newly confirmed case of Bundibugyo virus disease, according to a summary of the clinical trial posted on the National Institutes of Health website. A healthcare worker in protective clothing is disinfected by a colleague inside a treatment center for Ebola patients in Beni, North Kivu province, eastern Congo, Sept. 26, 2026. Sebastien Kitsa Musayi/AP Photo On day 1 of the trial, participants are randomized to receive either a BDBV vaccine candidate or standard treatment, according to the clinical trial summary. On day 29, all participants receive the alternative intervention so that every participant is offered a candidate vaccine while allowing the comparison between groups to remain blinded. Popular Reads Dollar General sued by FTC, 20 states for what's being described as deceptive pricing 1 day ago Britt McHenry resigns from State Department after basketball game altercation Oct 23 Judge rules Trump DOJ 'unlawfully' fired Maurene Comey 1 day ago An independent data monitoring committee will review data accumulated during the trial while additional vaccine candidates are added to the evaluation and others are discontinued if they are shown to be ineffective. "The overall aim is to generate robust evidence to support the use of BDBV vaccines for outbreak response and future epidemic preparedness," the summary states. Related Nigerian medical groups call for nationwide measures after 1st Ebola case MSF said Monday that the first vaccine candidate being studied is ERVEBO, which was developed and licensed to protect against the Zaire ebolavirus, which is the deadliest Ebola strain. At least two Bundibugyo-specific vaccine candidates being developed by Moderna and the University of Oxford in collaboration with the Serum Institute of India, and funded by CEPI, will be tested, according to MSF, which also said the Tumaini trial will notably include children. "Children are among those hardest hit by the Ebola outbreak in certain regions, yet they have historically been underrepresented in clinical research," said Valérie Briand, head of MSF's research division, Epicentre. "The inclusion of pregnant women and children aged 12 years and older, as well as children from one year of age for the ERVEBO vaccine, represents an important step toward generating evidence for populations that are often excluded from clinical trials." View five news elements
Time
On October 27, 2026, the first person was inoculated in a clinical trial for an Ebola vaccine. The trial's timeline includes day 1 and day 29, with participants receiving either a vaccine candidate or standard treatment on day 1, and all participants receiving the alternative intervention on day 29.
Location and population
The clinical trial is taking place in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically in Ituri. Participants are contacts of patients who were newly confirmed cases of Bundibugyo virus disease within the previous 21 days of enrollment.
Event
The first person was inoculated in a clinical trial for a vaccine targeting the Bundibugyo ebolavirus, which is responsible for the ongoing outbreak in the DRC. The trial is being conducted by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale in the DRC and the University of Antwerp's Global Health Institute in Belgium, with support from Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF).
Scale, impact and risk
The DRC outbreak caused by Bundibugyo ebolavirus has resulted in more than 10,000 cases and nearly 5,000 deaths. There are currently no vaccines or treatments available for the Bundibugyo virus. The trial aims to evaluate the safety, efficacy, and immunogenicity of several vaccine candidates, including ERVEBO, which is designed for Zaire ebolavirus.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Tumaini clinical trial is evaluating multiple vaccine candidates for Bundibugyo virus. An independent data monitoring committee will review the trial data, and additional vaccine candidates will be added or discontinued based on their effectiveness. The trial includes children and pregnant women, which is significant for outbreak response.

埃博拉:图迈尼临床试验中第一人接种疫苗 - 无国界医生组织

Ebola: First person vaccinated in Tumaini clinical trial - Doctors Without Borders
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 26 日,疫苗 候选药物针对本迪布焦病毒的 Tumaini 临床试验的第一位参与者接种了疫苗,暴发 在刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 继续进行。该试验是当前 Bundibugyo 暴发 持续应对措施的一部分,是迄今为止第二大埃博拉疾病 暴发。刚果民主共和国正面临有史以来最大的 Bundibugyo 暴发,目前还没有批准的针对该病毒的 疫苗。Tumaini 2/3 期临床试验旨在评估 疫苗 候选药物的安全性、免疫原性和有效性,以缩小这一差距。该试验由国家生物医学研究所和安特卫普大学全球健康研究所共同领导和共同赞助,其他合作伙伴包括刚果卫生部、流行病防范创新联盟、非洲疾病预防控制中心、世界卫生组织(WHO)和热带医学研究所。它将与受影响的社区、卫生当局和 暴发 应对合作伙伴密切合作,生成有关 疫苗 表现的有力证据,这可能有助于遏制疫情、拯救生命并加强对未来 Bundibugyo 暴发 的准备。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: First person vaccinated in Tumaini clinical trial - Doctors Without Borders

News Brief
On October 26, 2026, the first participant in the Tumaini clinical trial of vaccine candidates against Bundibugyo virus was vaccinated, as the outbreak continues in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). The trial, which is part of the ongoing response to the current Bundibugyo outbreak, is the second-largest Ebola disease outbreak to date. The DRC is facing the largest Bundibugyo outbreak ever recorded, and there is currently no approved vaccine against this virus. The Tumaini Phase 2/3 clinical trial aims to evaluate the safety, immunogenicity, and efficacy of vaccine candidates to close this gap. The trial is co-led and co-sponsored by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale and the University of Antwerp’s Global Health Institute, with other partners including the Congolese Ministry of Health, the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, Africa CDC, the World Health Organization (WHO), and the Institute of Tropical Medicine. It will work closely with affected communities, health authorities, and outbreak response partners to generate robust evidence on vaccine performance that could contribute to curtailing the epidemic, saving lives, and strengthening preparedness for future Bundibugyo outbreaks.

See latest updates Ebola: First person vaccinated in Tumaini clinical trial The Tumaini clinical trial aims to evaluate the safety and efficacy of vaccine candidates specific to Bundibugyo virus, which is driving the current Ebola outbreak in DR Congo. October 26, 2026 Share Share in FacebookShare in BlueSkyShare in LinkedInShare in TwitterShare in Mail A team member at MSF's Ebola treatment center near Kitatumba Hospital in Butembo, North Kivu province. | DR Congo 2026 © Lucie Ringué/MSF Related Democratic Republic of Congo Ebola Vaccination Outbreaks and epidemics View more Related Democratic Republic of Congo Ebola Vaccination Outbreaks and epidemics View more The first participant in the Tumaini clinical trial of vaccine candidates against Bundibugyo virus, which causes Ebola disease, was vaccinated today, as the outbreak continues in Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). Doctors Without Borders/Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF) and its research arm, Epicentre, are among several partners involved in this trial, which takes place in Bunia, Ituri province — the early epicenter of the outbreak. DRC is facing the largest Bundibugyo outbreak ever recorded, and the second-largest Ebola disease outbreak to date. There is currently no approved vaccine against Bundibugyo, which is one of several viruses causing Ebola disease. The Tumaini Phase 2/3 clinical trial aims to close that gap by evaluating the safety, immunogenicity, and efficacy of vaccine candidates. One of the candidates, ERVEBO, was developed and licensed to protect against the Zaire ebolavirus — not Bundibugyo, which is the virus driving the current outbreak. Marie Sembu, a nurse with the Congolese Ministry of Health, fills out an ERVEBO vaccination card for Dr. Anne Mushiya during the launch of the BRAVO clinical trial in Bunia. | DR Congo 2026 © Kristen Poels/MSF Trial is a critical step in the ongoing Ebola response “The Tumaini trial represents a critical step toward addressing the urgent needs of people most affected by Bundibugyo virus,” said MSF medical director Dr. Bern-Thomas Nyang'wa. “MSF brings to this trial its experience doing clinical research in close collaboration with affected communities. Working in partnership with communities is essential for building trust and ensuring meaningful participation in the development of vaccines that are effective, adapted, and accessible to those who need them most.” Tumaini will work closely with affected communities, health authorities, and outbreak response partners to generate robust evidence on vaccine performance that could contribute to curtailing the epidemic, saving lives, and strengthening preparedness for future Bundibugyo outbreaks. The trial is co-led and co-sponsored by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale and the University of Antwerp’s Global Health Institute. Other partners involved include the Congolese Ministry of Health, the Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, Africa CDC, the World Health Organization (WHO), and the Institute of Tropical Medicine. Working in partnership with communities is essential for building trust and ensuring meaningful participation in the development of vaccines that are effective, adapted, and accessible to those who need them most. Dr. Bern-Thomas Nyang'wa, MSF physician This clinical trial allows several vaccine candidates to be evaluated simultaneously, which can help shorten the time it takes to determine whether a vaccine is effective and can potentially be approved for wider use. Vaccines will be administered through a ring vaccination approach, which focuses on people who have had contact with someone with Ebola and those at highest risk of infection. Technical details about Tumaini are available on ClinicalTrials.gov under the trial number NCT07842107. Vaccine candidates are selected through an independent prioritization process led by WHO’s Technical Advisory Group on Candidate Vaccines Prioritization, and corresponding protocol amendments are approved by ACOREP, the national regulatory authority. The first candidate being used in the trial is ERVEBO. Preparations are underway for two Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations (CEPI)-funded Bundibugyo-specific vaccine candidates that are being developed by Moderna and the University of Oxford in collaboration with the Serum Institute of India. Furaha, age 3, prepares to return home after recovering from Ebola disease at MSF's Ebola treatment center in Mongbwalu, Ituri province. | DR Congo 2026 © Alexis Huguet/MSF Including children in Ebola research and development Tumaini, which means “hope” in Swahili, also represents an important step toward protecting children. While Ebola remains a deadly disease for all age groups, children face the highest risk of death, yet they are often excluded from related clinical research or included only after significant delays. This means they often get access to medical tools far too late, if at all. The Tumaini trial notably includes children. “Children are among those hardest hit by the Ebola outbreak in certain regions, yet they have historically been underrepresented in clinical research,” said Valérie Briand, head of the research department at MSF’s Epicentre. “The inclusion of pregnant women and children aged 12 years and older, as well as children from one year of age for the ERVEBO vaccine, represents an important step toward generating evidence for populations that are often excluded from clinical trials. Discussions are ongoing to extend the inclusion of younger children for specific vaccine candidates.” The Tumaini trial complements other ongoing vaccine trials and studies, including BRAVO, which MSF is also involved in and which focuses solely on determining the efficacy of ERVEBO against Bundibugyo. 图迈尼试验开始在刚果民主共和国为参与者接种疫苗 - FindArticles

Tumaini Trial Begins Vaccinating Participants in DR Congo - FindArticles
新闻简报Tumaini 疫苗 试验旨在评估现有的埃博拉 疫苗 是否可以预防本迪布焦病毒病,该试验于 2026 年 10 月 25 日开始在刚果民主共和国布尼亚为参与者接种疫苗。据半岛电视台报道并经 MedicalXpress 证实,第一批志愿者接受了 Ervebo,这是一种获得扎伊尔埃博拉病毒许可的 疫苗。该试验不是常规的 疫苗接种 活动,而是在活跃的 暴发 期间针对高危人群(包括孕妇和 12 岁及以上儿童)的重点研究。目前尚未发布安全性、免疫反应或疗效结果,试验结果将取决于观察到的感染数量和时间。该研究由国家生物医学研究所和安特卫普大学全球健康研究所牵头,CEPI 为共同发起者,世界卫生组织提供战略指导。该试验旨在评估 疫苗 在预防本迪布焦病毒病方面的有效性,但其结果仍有待确定。
查看新闻五要素
时间
Tumaini 试验于 2026 年 10 月 25 日开始为刚果民主共和国的参与者接种疫苗,10 月 24 日的报告显示第一批志愿者接种了 Ervebo。没有提供 疫苗接种 启动的具体日期,但该试验的启动被描述为最近的进展。
地点与对象
审判在刚果民主共和国伊图里省的布尼亚进行。它涉及接触本迪布焦病毒病的高风险参与者,包括孕妇和 12 岁及以上的儿童,但详细的资格标准尚未公布。
事件
图迈尼试验开始为刚果民主共和国(特别是布尼亚)的参与者接种疫苗,作为评估现有埃博拉 疫苗 是否可以预防本迪布焦病毒病的研究的一部分。这是 暴发 活跃期间的一个研究里程碑,而不是 疫苗 功效的证据。
规模、影响与风险
该试验旨在评估 疫苗 针对本迪布焦病毒病的安全性、免疫原性和潜在功效。尚未发布安全性、免疫反应或功效结果。该试验不是常规的 疫苗接种 活动,而是针对高危人群的重点研究。
应对、状态与不确定性
审判正在进行中,尚未公布结果。它由国家生物医学研究所和安特卫普大学全球健康研究所领导,刚果卫生部作为国家牵头单位。CEPI 作为共同发起者参与其中,WHO 提供战略、技术和科学建议。试验的结果取决于研究期间观察到的感染数量和时间。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Tumaini Trial Begins Vaccinating Participants in DR Congo - FindArticles

News Brief
The Tumaini vaccine trial, aimed at assessing whether an existing Ebola vaccine can protect against Bundibugyo virus disease, began vaccinating participants in Bunia, DR Congo, on October 25, 2026. The first volunteers received Ervebo, a vaccine licensed for Zaire ebolavirus, as reported by Al Jazeera and corroborated by MedicalXpress. This trial is not a routine vaccination campaign but a focused study on high-risk individuals, including pregnant women and children 12 and older, during an active outbreak. No safety, immune-response, or efficacy results have been released yet, and the trial's outcomes will depend on the number and timing of infections observed. The study is led by the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale and the University of Antwerp’s Global Health Institute, with CEPI as a co-sponsor and WHO providing strategic guidance. The trial is designed to evaluate the vaccine's effectiveness in preventing Bundibugyo virus disease, but its results remain pending.
Original source text (English)
News Technology Business Entertainment Science & Health Knowledge Base Font ResizerAa Search News Technology Business Entertainment Science & Health Knowledge Base Follow US Tumaini Trial Begins Vaccinating Participants in DR Congo Last updated: October 25, 2026 12:36 am By Pam Belluck Science & Health 7 Min Read SHARE First participants in the Tumaini vaccine trial have reportedly been vaccinated in Bunia, in the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Ituri province, opening a study meant to answer a still-unresolved question: whether an existing Ebola vaccine can protect people against Bundibugyo virus disease. Several reports published Oct. 24 said the first volunteers received Ervebo, a vaccine licensed for Ebola disease caused by Zaire ebolavirus. The launch, reported by Al Jazeera and corroborated by MedicalXpress in an AFP report, is a research milestone during an active outbreak, not evidence that the vaccine works against Bundibugyo virus. No safety, immune-response or efficacy results from Tumaini have been released. Table of Contents Why Ervebo is being studied How Tumaini is designed to work A vaccine study, separate from the treatment trial What evidence the trial could provide Why Ervebo is being studied Bundibugyo virus is distinct from Zaire ebolavirus, even though both are among the viruses that can cause Ebola disease. A vaccine’s protection against one virus cannot be assumed to extend to another; cross-protection has to be demonstrated in study results. In May, a World Health Organization expert review said no vaccine or treatment had been specifically approved for Bundibugyo virus disease. It described the evidence that Ervebo might protect against Bundibugyo virus as limited and inconclusive, and advised that its use for that purpose be evaluated in carefully designed research. That is the purpose of Tumaini, whose name means “hope” in Swahili. The trial is not a routine outbreak vaccination campaign. It is designed to establish whether vaccination has measurable benefits against Bundibugyo virus disease and to gather the safety and immunogenicity evidence needed to interpret any protection signal. Ervebo is expected to be the first vaccine tested. The Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations, or CEPI, said Oct. 13 that two vaccines tailored to Bundibugyo virus were also in early-stage studies: an mRNA candidate from Moderna and a ChAdOx1 candidate developed by the University of Oxford and the Serum Institute of India. Those candidates could be added to Tumaini when available. How Tumaini is designed to work CEPI’s description of Tumaini calls it a pivotal Phase 2/3 adaptive platform trial. Rather than creating a separate full trial for every product, a platform can assess more than one candidate under a shared framework as new vaccines become ready for study. Tumaini uses ring vaccination, directing study vaccination toward people at high risk after an identified exposure. The study uses a ring-vaccination approach, focusing on contacts considered at high risk of infection. Such rings can include people exposed to a person with confirmed illness and others with close connections to that exposure. The approach directs study activity toward people most likely to encounter the virus, which can be especially important when cases are geographically clustered. CEPI says the trial is intended to assess safety, immunogenicity—the body’s immune response—and efficacy, or whether vaccination reduces disease among people at risk. But the public descriptions do not give a planned sample size, detailed endpoint definitions, statistical analysis plan or the trial’s allocation and comparator arrangements. Those omissions make it too early to infer how quickly a reliable efficacy answer may emerge. News reports say Tumaini will include pregnant women and children 12 and older. Detailed eligibility criteria have not been published in the material describing the launch. The trial is led by the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale and the University of Antwerp’s Global Health Institute, with the Congolese Ministry of Health serving as national lead. CEPI identifies the two research institutions as co-sponsors; WHO is providing strategic, technical and scientific advice rather than sponsoring the study. A vaccine study, separate from the treatment trial Tumaini follows another clinical study in the country, but the two trials address different questions. In July, WHO announced enrollment in PARTNERS, a randomized therapeutic trial for people already confirmed to have Bundibugyo virus disease. That study is evaluating the experimental antibody treatment MBP134 and the antiviral remdesivir. The WHO announcement of PARTNERS described a search for treatments that could improve outcomes after infection. Tumaini, by contrast, is testing prevention among people at heightened risk of exposure. A favorable result in one study would not establish that the intervention in the other works. Outbreak totals have varied in public accounts over the course of the year, with reports citing different dates and case definitions. The important point for Tumaini’s scientific purpose is that an active outbreak can provide the exposures needed to assess whether a vaccine changes disease risk. It can also complicate the work: transmission patterns, access to affected communities and the pace of case detection may all affect how much evidence accumulates. What evidence the trial could provide A Phase 2/3 label does not mean a vaccine has been shown to work. Phase 2 research commonly examines safety and immune responses, while Phase 3 evidence is typically used to assess whether an intervention prevents disease. Tumaini’s adaptive structure is intended to move candidates through those questions in an outbreak setting, but its results will depend on the number and timing of infections observed during the study. For Ervebo, the trial may clarify whether an immune response developed for Zaire ebolavirus has useful cross-protection against Bundibugyo virus. For the newer candidates, it could provide a route to compare products designed specifically for the circulating virus. Neither possibility is a result yet. The first vaccinations therefore mark the beginning of evidence gathering, not a change in the regulatory status of any vaccine for Bundibugyo virus disease. The study’s eventual value will rest on the outcome data it produces: whether vaccinated high-risk participants show protection, how durable any protection is, and whether the safety findings support use in the populations enrolled. She has reported extensively on topics like reproductive health, long-term illness, brain science, and public health, with a focus on both complex medical developments and human-centered narratives. Her writing bridges investigative depth with accessible storytelling, often covering issues at the intersection of science, ethics, and personal experience. Location and population
The trial is taking place in Bunia, located in the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Ituri province. It involves participants at high risk of exposure to Bundibugyo virus disease, including pregnant women and children 12 and older, though detailed eligibility criteria are not yet published.
Event
The Tumaini trial began vaccinating participants in DR Congo, specifically in Bunia, as part of a study to evaluate whether an existing Ebola vaccine can protect against Bundibugyo virus disease. This is a research milestone during an active outbreak, not evidence of vaccine efficacy.
Scale, impact and risk

刚果民主共和国关键 2/3 期 Bundibugyo 疫苗 试验中第一位参与者接种了疫苗 - CEPI

First participant vaccinated in pivotal Phase 2/3 Bundibugyo vaccine trial in DRC - CEPI
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 23 日,在刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 布尼亚进行的针对本迪布焦病毒 (BDBV) 的关键 2/3 期临床试验 (Tumaini) 中,第一位参与者接种了疫苗。该试验由刚果民主共和国卫生部牵头,并得到 CEPI、世卫组织和非洲疾病预防控制中心的支持,旨在评估 疫苗 候选药物,包括 Ervebo,该药物已获许可用于对抗 埃博拉病毒 (EBOV)。刚果民主共和国目前面临着有记录以来最大规模的 BDBV 暴发,截至 2026 年 10 月 21 日,已有 9,552 例确诊病例和 4,602 例死亡。该试验使用自适应平台和 环形疫苗接种 方法来评估 疫苗 的安全性、免疫原性和有效性。它旨在支持政府主导的对 暴发 的响应,并加强对未来 BDBV 暴发 的准备。该试验是更广泛努力的一部分,旨在满足受影响社区的迫切需求,并确保研究与一线应对活动密切协调。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

First participant vaccinated in pivotal Phase 2/3 Bundibugyo vaccine trial in DRC - CEPI

News Brief
On 23 October 2026, the first participant was vaccinated in a pivotal Phase 2/3 clinical trial (Tumaini) against Bundibugyo virus (BDBV) in Bunia, Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). This trial, led by the DRC Ministry of Health and supported by CEPI, WHO, and Africa CDC, aims to evaluate vaccine candidates, including Ervebo, which is licensed for use against Ebola virus (EBOV). The DRC is currently facing the largest recorded BDBV outbreak, with 9,552 confirmed cases and 4,602 deaths as of 21 October 2026. The trial uses an adaptive platform and ring vaccination approach to assess vaccine safety, immunogenicity, and efficacy. It is designed to support the Government-led response to the outbreak and strengthen preparedness for future BDBV outbreaks. The trial is part of a broader effort to address the urgent needs of affected communities and ensure that research is closely coordinated with frontline response activities.
Original source text (English)
Securing the future: CEPI's 3.0 Investment Case Find out moreDismiss About What we do Partner with us Our impact News & stories Contact us News First participant vaccinated in pivotal Phase 2/3 Bundibugyo vaccine trial in DRC 23rd October 2026 Share Tumaini, a pivotal Phase 2/3 clinical trial, will evaluate vaccine candidates including Ervebo as part of the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s (DRC) outbreak response. There is currently no approved vaccine against Bundibugyo virus. Tumaini aims to close that gap by generating robust evidence about the performance of multiple vaccine candidates. BUNIA, DEMOCRATIC REPUBLIC OF THE CONGO, 23 October 2026 – The first participant has been vaccinated in Tumaini, a pivotal Phase 2/3 clinical trial of vaccine candidates against Bundibugyo virus (BDBV). The vaccination, administered on 23 October 2026 in Bunia, DRC marks the start of the trial, which will support the Government-led response to the ongoing outbreak. The trial begins as the DRC faces the largest recorded BDBV outbreak and the second-largest Ebola outbreak to date. The outbreak was officially declared on 15 May 2026 and was determined as a Public Health Emergency of International Concern by the World Health Organization (WHO) and a Public Health Emergency of Continental Security by Africa CDC in the days thereafter. As of 21 October 2026, 9,552 cases and 4,602 deaths had been confirmed in the DRC [1]. There is currently no approved vaccine to protect against BDBV. Tumaini, which means “hope” in Swahili, aims to close that gap by evaluating the safety, immunogenicity and efficacy of vaccine candidates, including Ervebo which is currently licensed for use to prevent Ebola virus (EBOV). Tumaini will work closely with affected communities, health authorities and outbreak response partners to generate robust evidence on vaccine performance that could contribute to curtailing the epidemic, saving lives, and strengthening preparedness for future BDBV outbreaks. Prof. Steve Ahuka-Mundeke, Incident Manager of the Ebola Response, Democratic Republic of the Congo, said: “Bringing this trial into the Ebola response is an important step towards addressing the urgent needs of affected communities. Our priority is to stop transmission and save lives, while ensuring that research is closely coordinated with frontline response activities. We thank the participants, communities and partners whose trust and engagement make it possible to evaluate potential new tools against Bundibugyo virus.” Tumaini is an adaptive platform trial, which allows several vaccine candidates to be evaluated in parallel within a single framework and saves valuable time during an outbreak. Candidates will be assessed through ring vaccination, which focuses on contacts at high risk of infection. Technical details are available on ClinicalTrials.gov (NCT07842107). Vaccine candidates are selected through an independent prioritisation process led by the WHO Technical Advisory Group on Candidate Vaccine Prioritisation (TAG-CVP), and corresponding protocol amendments are approved by ACOREP, the national regulatory authority. The first candidate to enter the trial is Ervebo. Preparations are underway for two CEPI-funded BDBV-specific vaccine candidates, under development by Moderna and by the University of Oxford in collaboration with Serum Institute of India, to enter the trial shortly. The Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale (INRB) and the University of Antwerp (Global Health Institute) co-lead and co-sponsor the trial. The DRC Ministry of Health provides national leadership and oversight. A Trial Steering Committee, chaired by the Ministry’s Incident Response Manager, provides strategic oversight and aligns the trial with the wider outbreak response and with other studies of medical countermeasures in the region. Prof. Hugo Kavunga, Tumaini Co-Sponsor and Principal Investigator, Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale, said: “Vaccinating the first participant is an important milestone, made possible by the dedication of Congolese researchers, health workers and our partners. With affected communities at the heart of this effort, we are placing national scientific leadership at the service of the outbreak response. Our priority is to establish which vaccines can safely and effectively protect those at risk.” Prof. Jean-Pierre Van geertruyden, Tumaini Co-Sponsor and Co-Principal Investigator, University of Antwerp, said: “During an outbreak, speed and scientific rigour must go hand in hand. Tumaini brings together Congolese and international expertise to generate reliable evidence on vaccines against Bundibugyo virus. Through this partnership, we aim to support the response today while building the knowledge needed to protect communities in future outbreaks.” Africa CDC plays a strategic role in advancing African-led clinical research, strengthening regional coordination, ensuring that emerging scientific evidence informs public health decision-making and the wider Ebola outbreak response, and supporting timely and equitable access to successful vaccine candidates. Through the R&D Blueprint, WHO has developed protocols for filovirus vaccine studies that were adapted rapidly for the Tumaini trial. WHO provides leadership in the BVD response supporting the Ministry of Health of the DRC. For the Tumaini trial, WHO provides strategic, technical and scientific advice along with on-the-ground experience to stand up a vaccine trial embedded in an emergency response. CEPI provides funding, scientific expertise and operational support and is backing a portfolio of BDBV-specific vaccine candidates that could be evaluated in the study. Partners including Epicentre, Médecins Sans Frontières (MSF), the Institute of Tropical Medicine Antwerp (ITM), WHO and Africa CDC support implementation of the trial. MSF uses the organisation’s own funding and operational capacity to support the trial. Tumaini is funded by CEPI’s Bundibugyo virus vaccine development programme, which, to date, is backed by the European Commission, the U.S. Department of State, the Fund for Science and Technology, the Republic of Korea and CEPI’s core funding. Beyond the trial, Tumaini is part of a broader “push–pull” partnership between CEPI and Gavi, the Vaccine Alliance, in which Gavi has committed up to US$40 million to fund manufacturing readiness to ensure vaccines are available at scale as soon as they are approved for deployment. Dr Richard Hatchett, CEO of CEPI, said: “There is still no licensed vaccine against Bundibugyo virus, and the cost of that absence is being counted in the DRC every day, in lives lost and in health workers who go to work without protection. Tumaini aims to change that. Today’s first vaccination opens a pivotal trial to generate the evidence needed to get safe and effective vaccines to the people who need them most.” Dr Chikwe Ihekweazu, Executive Director, Health Emergencies Programme, World Health Organization, said: "The launch of the Tumaini trial marks a critical step towards establishing whether candidate vaccines can effectively protect people against the Bundibugyo virus. This is much more than a research study. The trial reflects WHO’s strong recommendation to evaluate candidate vaccines through well-designed research during outbreaks, using a single adaptive platform and a ring vaccination approach. This is an approach WHO and countries have used in previous outbreaks to test vaccines, including the one that is effective against Ebola virus (previously known as Zaire ebolavirus). The trial is an opportunity to generate the rigorous evidence needed to determine whether these vaccines can protect people at risk. If successful, this would be the first time we have clear evidence of vaccine efficacy against Bundibugyo virus, a major advance that could transform how we respond to this outbreak and prepare for future outbreaks and help save lives." Prof. Placide Mbala, Director of Research, Clinical Trials and Innovation at Africa CDC, said : “Tumaini means hope, and today that hope is being translated into action. At a time when our communities are facing the largest recorded Bundibugyo outbreak, this trial demonstrates the power of African-led research to address African public health challenges. This is our collective commitment to generating high-quality evidence that can save lives today while strengthening preparedness for future epidemics.” Prof. Wim Adriaensen, Head of the Clinical Immunology Unit at ITM, said: “Through the long-standing partnership between INRB and ITM, we can quickly bring the required laboratory capacity to the affected region. This helps us identify the immune responses associated with vaccine protection. We can then compare vaccines that arrive late in, or even after, the outbreak with those protective immune responses, so promising candidates aren’t overlooked simply because they become available at a later stage.” Valérie Briand, Head of the Research Department, Epicentre, said: “Children are among those hardest hit by the Ebola outbreak in certain regions, yet they have historically been under-represented in clinical research. The inclusion of pregnant women and children aged 12 years and older, as well as children from one year of age for the Ervebo vaccine, represents an important step towards generating evidence for populations that are often excluded from clinical trials. Discussions are already ongoing to extend inclusion to younger children for specific vaccine candidates.” Bern-Thomas Nyang'wa, Medical Director, MSF, said: “The Tumaini trial represents a critical step towards addressing the urgent needs of people most affected by Bundibugyo virus. MSF brings to this trial its experience of doing clinical research in close collaboration with affected communities. Working in partnership with communities is essential for building trust and ensuring meaningful participation in the development of vaccines that are effective, adapted, and accessible to those who need them most.” [1] DRC Ebola Bundibugyo - Epidemic Intelligence Dashboard This press release is also available in French. ENDS Notes to editors The Institute of Tropical Medicine (ITM) is responsible for the laboratory activities and immunogenicity assessments that are essential for the registration and licensing of candidate vaccines. In addition, ITM contributes to research into the social and societal aspects of vaccination. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Congo Reports Over 10,000 Ebola Cases, Sparking Concerns of Wider Spread

News Brief
The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) has reported over 10,000 confirmed cases of Ebola virus disease, with nearly 4,000 deaths, raising concerns about the potential for wider spread across Africa. Health officials are worried that the outbreak may reach a critical point where cross-border transmission becomes difficult to contain. Countries like Kenya, Nigeria, and Uganda have also reported numerous cases in recent weeks, indicating the disease's growing reach. The World Health Organization notes that Ebola symptoms can be mistaken for other illnesses, and only laboratory testing can confirm the diagnosis. The CDC has issued quarantine guidelines for U.S. nationals who have been in the DRC, requiring them to stay outside the country for 21 days before entering the United States. Challenges such as weak health infrastructure, limited access to care, and local distrust in public health initiatives are complicating the response. Despite these difficulties, global and national health authorities are working to mobilize resources and manage the outbreak. The situation remains under investigation, with the potential for further escalation.

Cheesecake Factory drama 2026 midterm polls Bolsonaro leaves Brazil Powerball lottery Israeli election 3-year-old soccer phenom Voter ID rules Trump attacks El-Sayed Lula wins reelection in Brazil Follow Congo Reports Over 10,000 Ebola Cases, Sparking Concerns of Wider Spread Dr. Sai Balasubramanian, M.D., J.D., Contributor Updated Tue, October 27, 2026 at 9:29 PM UTC 2 min read Add Yahoo as a preferred source to see more of our stories on Google. Ebola is rapidly spreading across multiple countries in Africa. AFP via Getty Images The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) reported 10,000 confirmed cases of Ebola this week, with nearly 4000+ deaths. Health officials are now concerned that numbers may soon reach a point of inflection where cross-border spread and rampant infection throughout the continent may become impossible to contain. Other countries, including Kenya, Nigeria and Uganda have also reported numerous cases in recent weeks. Public health officials both at the global level and national front are rushing to mobilize care for those affected in addition to public health resources to help manage the rapid spread of the disease. This week, the United States Centers for Disease Control and Prevention also provided an update regarding the evolving situation: "This outbreak is spreading substantially faster than previous Ebola outbreaks and is now the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record.... [and] The response to this outbreak is challenging due to a number of complex circumstances in the region." Some of the complexities the agency cites include challenges in health infrastructure and access to care locally, ongoing violence and conflict across certain regions, and consistent lack of trust in public health initiatives. Advertisement Advertisement Advertisement Advertisement Governments including the United States have also issued strict quarantine and return to home guidelines for travelers: "All U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in DRC should plan to remain outside DRC for 21 days before entering the United States. This includes all passengers with an intermediate stop in DRC or on multi-stop itineraries via DRC regardless of whether they disembark or stay on the airplane." The World Health Organization explains that Ebola symptoms can be somewhat elusive and easily confused for other common ailments; standard symptoms include "fever, fatigue, muscle pain, headache and sore throat...vomiting, diarrhoea, rash, internal and external bleeding etc." It also explains that timelines and testing are important aspects of confirmatory diagnosis: "The time interval from infection to onset of symptoms ranges from 2 to 21 days....Even when people have developed Ebola disease-like symptoms, only a laboratory test can confirm whether the cause is Ebola." Unfortunately, treatment options are typically limited and Ebola is notorious for high mortality rates. The best way for patients to be safe is to ensure that they do not expose themselves to the virus or to people that may be transmitting the virus. Although Ebola breakouts by no means are new, this is a rapidly evolving situation that requires close attention from the international public heath community. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

尼日利亚埃博拉病毒感染源之谜引发人们对病毒传播的担忧 - 《卫报》

Mystery over source of Ebola infection in Nigeria fuels concern about spread of virus - The Guardian
新闻简报2026 年 10 月,尼日利亚确认了十多年来首例埃博拉病例,涉及一名瑞士外交官出生于乌干达的妻子。她的感染源正在调查中,相互矛盾的报道表明她可能是在乌干达或刚果民主共和国旅行时感染了该病毒。卫生官员正在努力通过接触者追踪来遏制 暴发,而世界卫生组织则继续探索可能的 传播 路线。由本迪布焦病毒引起的 暴发 疫情已蔓延至四个非洲国家,是刚果民主共和国历史上规模最大、死亡人数最多的疫情,已有 9,500 多人感染,其中近一半病例导致死亡。官员们强调,由于情况仍然不确定且正在调查中,因此需要快速反应和跨境合作,以防止进一步传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年10月,尼日利亚报告了暴发病例,患者感染源正在调查中。据信该患者在乌干达旅行时感染了该病毒,证据中没有明确说明事件日期。
地点与对象
尼日利亚,特别是拉各斯,该患者是在那里被发现的。患者是驻阿布贾一名瑞士外交官的妻子,暴发 已传播到多个非洲国家,包括乌干达、肯尼亚,现在又增加到尼日利亚。
事件
尼日利亚确认了十多年来首例埃博拉病例,患者是一名瑞士外交官出生于乌干达的妻子。感染源正在调查中,关于她的旅行史的报道相互矛盾。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 是刚果民主共和国历史上规模最大、死亡人数最多的一次,感染人数超过 9,500 例,其中近一半导致死亡。尼日利亚病例被认为已得到控制,但疫情蔓延到第四个非洲国家引发了人们对进一步 传播 的担忧。
应对、状态与不确定性
尼日利亚卫生官员正在进行接触者追踪,以控制 暴发。世界卫生组织正在调查感染源,并且正在讨论与肯尼亚和乌干达的潜在联系。目前情况仍在调查中,具体感染源尚不确定。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Mystery over source of Ebola infection in Nigeria fuels concern about spread of virus - The Guardian

News Brief
In October 2026, Nigeria confirmed its first Ebola case in over a decade, involving the Ugandan-born wife of a Swiss diplomat. The source of her infection is under investigation, with conflicting reports suggesting she may have contracted the virus while traveling in Uganda or the Democratic Republic of Congo. Health officials are working to contain the outbreak through contact-tracing, while the World Health Organization continues to explore the likely transmission route. The outbreak, caused by the Bundibugyo virus, has spread to four African countries and is the largest and deadliest in DR Congo’s history, with over 9,500 infections and nearly half of the cases resulting in deaths. Officials emphasize the need for rapid response and cross-border collaboration to prevent further spread, as the situation remains uncertain and under investigation.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Sodiq Adelakun/Reuters View image in fullscreen Travellers stand next to an Ebola awareness banner at Murtala Muhammed international airport in Lagos, Nigeria. Photograph: Sodiq Adelakun/Reuters Mystery over source of Ebola infection in Nigeria fuels concern about spread of virus Switzerland says patient is wife of Swiss diplomat and believed to have contracted virus while travelling in Uganda Health officials across Africa are racing to determine how Nigeria’s first Ebola patient in more than a decade became infected, amid fears the outbreak could spread further after reaching a fourth African country. The Swiss foreign ministry said on Friday the patient in Nigeria, the Ugandan-born wife of a Swiss diplomat based in Abuja, had not travelled to the Democratic Republic of Congo, where the outbreak was first detected in mid-May before spreading to Uganda, Kenya and now Nigeria. The woman was believed to have contracted Ebola while travelling with her husband in Uganda, the ministry added. The new information contradicts accounts from Uganda, which previously said the woman had travelled to eastern DR Congo, and will raise concerns about the ability of authorities to contain the outbreak, which is already the most geographically widespread in history. The Ugandan government reported Ebola cases in May and June before WHO declared it Ebola-free in August. A Ugandan government spokesperson told Reuters on Thursday that Uganda had no ​Ebola cases and that, if ​it did, it would make that information ​public. The World Health Organization said investigations were continuing to establish the likely source of infection for the Ebola patient in Nigeria. The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention is investigating whether the patient may have come into contact with an Ebola patient in Kenya, as both travelled from Uganda’s Entebbe airport on 3 October, said Dr Wessam Mankoula, head of emergency preparedness and response at the organisation. Catherine Smallwood, a WHO Ebola incident manager in DR Congo, told a UN briefing in Geneva via video link on Friday that WHO staff in DR Congo had no information showing the patient in Nigeria had travelled to the country. Smallwood said a link to the case in Kenya was possible, but added that officials would need to compare the timing with when the Nigeria patient’s symptoms began to tell whether the infection more likely happened at the Ugandan airport or elsewhere. Marion Koopmans, a Dutch virologist and member of the WHO’s Ebola emergency committee, cast doubt on the theory. “I think that is very speculative unless there is evidence of sharing the same space in a manner that could explain that,” she said. “Random presence at the same location, a large airport, is not a supported epidemiological link by any standard of what we currently know.” The Nigerian health minister, Muhammad Ali Pate, said Nigeria’s case remains contained and that contact-tracing was continuing. “We are trying to contain it so it doesn’t go out to the communities. Hopefully, it won’t get to that and we are working hard to ensure that,” Pate said. Melaku Yirga, vice-president for Africa at Prosper Global (formerly Mercy Corps), said the confirmation of Ebola in Kenya and Nigeria reinforces the need for countries to work closely together to prevent further transmission. “What matters most now is speed, sharing information across borders without delay and ensuring frontline health workers and local organiaations have the resources to respond quickly,” he said. Ebola is a highly contagious and often fatal viral disease that affects humans and non-human primates. It spreads through body fluids or contaminated materials and causes organ damage, blood vessel impairment and sometimes severe internal and external bleeding. The current outbreak is caused by the rare Bundibugyo virus, which has no approved treatment or vaccine. It was first announced on 15 May and has infected more than 9,500 people and killed nearly half of them. The outbreak is the largest and deadliest in DR Congo’s history and second only globally to the 2014-16 outbreak in west Africa, which infected more than 28,600 people and killed more than 11,000. Nigeria, specifically Lagos, where the patient was identified. The patient is the wife of a Swiss diplomat based in Abuja, and the outbreak has spread to multiple African countries including Uganda, Kenya, and now Nigeria.
Event
Nigeria confirmed its first Ebola case in over a decade, with the patient being the Ugandan-born wife of a Swiss diplomat. The source of infection is under investigation, and there are conflicting reports about her travel history.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak is the largest and deadliest in DR Congo’s history, with over 9,500 infections and nearly half of them resulting in deaths. The Nigeria case is considered contained, but the spread to a fourth African country raises concerns about further transmission.
Response, status and uncertainty

The World Health Organization is investigating the source of infection, and there are ongoing discussions about the potential link to Kenya and Uganda. 肯尼亚20名埃博拉病例接触者解除隔离,刚果(金)疫苗试验带来新希望

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

世界卫生组织表示,肯尼亚首例本迪布焦型埃博拉病毒病例的20名接触者,在完成21天隔离监测后,于周一解除隔离并与家人团聚。另有98名接触者仍在隔离中。与此同时,刚果民主共和国上周五启动一项临床试验,评估疫苗能否预防本迪布焦型埃博拉病毒病。目前,针对这一疾病尚无获批疫苗或特效疗法。
